		<item>
		<title>Detachable Fiber Connectors Pose New Challenges For Photonics</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/detachable-fiber-connectors-pose-new-challenges-for-photonics/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/detachable-fiber-connectors-pose-new-challenges-for-photonics/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Bryon Moyer]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 19 Nov 2026 08:01:36 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Manufacturing, Packaging and Materials]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Top Stories]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AIM Photonics]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amkor]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Ayar Labs]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[co-packaged optics]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[CPO]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[fiber attach]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intel Foundry]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Lightmatter]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Mitsubishi Chemical]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[near packaged optics]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[NPO]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[NY Creates]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[photonics]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Senko]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[STL]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Synopsys]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[University of Toronto]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24284397</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Multiple designs are emerging for CPO and NPO, but standardization and high-volume manufacturing are still not ready.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/detachable-fiber-connectors-pose-new-challenges-for-photonics/">Detachable Fiber Connectors Pose New Challenges For Photonics</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p><span style="font-weight: normal !msorm;"><strong>Key </strong></span><span style="font-weight: normal !msorm;"><strong>T</strong></span><span style="font-weight: normal !msorm;"><strong>ake</strong></span><strong>a<span style="font-weight: normal !msorm;">ways:</span></strong></p>
<ul>
<li>CPO and NPO require new approaches for connecting optical fibers to chip packages.</li>
<li>Detachable connectors can simplify assembly, test, and replacement of optical components.</li>
<li>Numerous bespoke designs exist today, but standardization hasn&#8217;t begun yet.</li>
</ul>
<hr />
<p>As photonics shifts from pluggable optical transceivers toward near- and co-packaged optics (NPO, CPO), engineers are devising new ways to attach fibers to the photonics chips on a board. Several different designs exist today, but so far there is no common interface.</p>
<p>With pluggable photonics, lasers and connections are attached at the server faceplate, with the board itself housing only electronic components. Yet long electrical connections to the faceplate mean higher power and longer latency. Fibers would need to move onto the interior of the board to solve that. That eliminates the need for pluggables, but it requires new solutions.</p>
<p>“With the rapid growth of generative AI, AI data centers are required to process enormous volumes of data faster and more efficiently than ever before,” said Yoshimasa Bando, principal scientist at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/mitsubishi-chemical-group/"><span style="font-weight: normal !msorm;">Mitsubishi Chemical</span></a>. “To meet this demand, further improvements in communication speed and energy efficiency have become critical challenges.”</p>
<p>The goal of CPO and NPO is to reduce reliance on long electrical connections.</p>
<p><strong>Long lines</strong><br />
One reason for maintaining faceplate pluggables is that lasers are not always reliable. Using a pluggable module makes it easy to replace the laser.</p>
<p>“What we are focused on has the laser on the faceplate, because the laser is the highest reliability risk,” said Vishal Chandrasekar, director of product management at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/ayar-labs/"><span style="font-weight: normal !msorm;">Ayar Labs</span></a>.</p>
<p>All connections, whether they have a laser or not, happen in pluggable modules today. That means the signals those fibers carry must travel electrically from their origin to the edge of the board. Bandwidth necessitates serial lines driven by serializer/deserializer (SerDes) circuits. Driving those signals over that distance requires high power.</p>
<p>CPO aims to reduce system power by eliminating those long electrical lines and bringing fiber into the processor or switch packages. &#8220;CPO unlocks the token economics that everyone cares about,&#8221; said Chandrasekar. &#8220;The major bottlenecks are compute, memory, and moving data back and forth. If you&#8217;re trying to make step changes in hardware every one or two years, the way you make 5× or 10× kinds of improvements is by having new hardware. How do you use that hardware intelligently? How do I ensure that whatever hardware I have, I&#8217;m using it for the most important problem so my utilization is good?&#8221;</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24284401" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.45.12-AM.png?resize=1184%2C1152&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="1184" height="1152" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.45.12-AM.png?w=1184&amp;ssl=1 1184w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.45.12-AM.png?resize=300%2C292&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.45.12-AM.png?resize=1024%2C996&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.45.12-AM.png?resize=768%2C747&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.45.12-AM.png?resize=600%2C584&amp;ssl=1 600w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.45.12-AM.png?resize=24%2C24&amp;ssl=1 24w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.45.12-AM.png?resize=48%2C48&amp;ssl=1 48w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /><br />
<strong>Fig. 1: With co-packaged optics, an optical engine is placed inside a package along with other chiplets. The laser remains outside the package for thermal reasons, and in the near term it will remain pluggable. Source: Bryon Moyer/Semiconductor Engineering</strong></p>
<p>Photonics plays a key role in this effort. “You&#8217;re trying to reduce copper, so you&#8217;re trying to bring the PIC [photonic integrated circuit] closer to the processor or the switch,” said David Harame, COO at AIM Photonics, which operates with <span style="font-weight: normal !msorm;">NY Creates </span>(New York Center for Research, Economic Advancement, Technology, Engineering, and Science). &#8220;The industry is still very much using pluggables; you plug in the front panel. So there&#8217;s a lot of copper going between this [and the processor]. The next step is to move the optical transceiver closer to the switch by putting it on the PCB and reducing the copper line length to the switch. This is called near-packaged optics. Essentially, it moves the optical pluggable connector further inside the box on the PC board, so the optical-to-electrical connection takes place closer to the switch.</p>
<p>CPO is yet another variant of advanced packaging. &#8220;And all the challenges in advanced packaging are the same for co-packaged optics, except for fiber coupling and getting the PIC closer to the target chip,” noted Harame.</p>
<p>CPO receives the bulk of the near-term optical development efforts.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24284403" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.48.59-AM.png?resize=969%2C1024&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="969" height="1024" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.48.59-AM.png?w=969&amp;ssl=1 969w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.48.59-AM.png?resize=284%2C300&amp;ssl=1 284w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.48.59-AM.png?resize=768%2C812&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.48.59-AM.png?resize=600%2C634&amp;ssl=1 600w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.48.59-AM.png?resize=24%2C24&amp;ssl=1 24w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 969px) 100vw, 969px" /><br />
<strong>Fig. 2: In near-packaged optics, the optical components lie on the board near where their signals originate. Lasers will remain pluggable for now. Source: Bryon Moyer/Semiconductor Engineering</strong></p>
<p><strong>Unknown fiber length</strong><br />
Both CPO and NPO share the need to connect fiber directly to components on the board. One straightforward way is to attach a nominal length of fiber to the chip package and ship it that way, with the system integrator connecting the other end of the fiber directly to the destination.</p>
<p>The challenge there is that chip producers have no way of knowing how long that fiber will need to be. If it&#8217;s long enough for designs that require long runs, it will be too long for the many designs that don’t. That said, there are ways of dealing with extra fiber.</p>
<p>&#8220;The fiber goes into a fiber management tray, and there are spool points on the board,” said Nick Harris, CEO of <span style="font-weight: normal !msorm;">Lightmatter</span>. “They&#8217;re neatly managed for airflow.”</p>
<p>Others agree. “Usually, you&#8217;ll have some kind of excess fiber management,” explained Obie Roy, vice president, strategic commercial development at <span style="font-weight: normal !msorm;">Senko</span>. “But depending on the system that you&#8217;re building, you could cut it to length and terminate it once you know the exact application.”</p>
<p>Some engineers see this as a non-issue, which may be true if one is fusing the fibers or having the integrator attach a connector. “The fiber length is irrelevant,” said Professor Amr Helmy at the University of Toronto. “You connect a strand of fiber to the chip. With the other end, you can connectorize it, cut it, or fuse it with another fiber to make it longer. Fusing is one approach, but each one of these fibers can come with a connector depending on the amount of loss appetite you have.”</p>
<p>For ease of assembly, some means of attaching and detaching a fiber would also be helpful, such as a fixture or clip on the end to which another fiber can attach. But several considerations make this decision about more than simply adding wire connections as shown in the figures above.</p>
<p><strong>Moving beyond the die edge</strong><br />
Today, fibers typically are placed using well-established V-groove technology, where the fibers exit out the side of a photonic chip. This attachment method works at low volumes and low total fiber counts, but it lacks the modularity and convenience needed for high-volume automation.</p>
<p>“The V-groove is a great concept, but the fibers are attached,” explained Gerard John, senior director, chiplets/FCBGA business unit at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/amkor-technology/"><span style="font-weight: normal !msorm;">Amkor</span></a>. “When being assembled onto a board, if one of them breaks, there&#8217;s no rework. People are generally thinking in terms of detachables and moving away from V-grooves.”</p>
<p>In addition, attachment space is bound by shoreline, which is essentially the real estate on the edge of the die. With metal connections, it might include the first few rows of bumps on the package, but fiber footprint is bigger and can chew up a lot of shoreline.</p>
<p>“The fiber diameter is 10 µm, but you have all the cladding around it, making it 125 µm,&#8221; John said. &#8220;So if you&#8217;re putting, let&#8217;s say, 16 cables like that, even if it&#8217;s staggered you will use a lot of real estate.&#8221;</p>
<p>Nevertheless, this is the predominant approach today. For example, Senko has put together a LEGO-like connector, with one part glued to the chip and the other attached to a fiber. This is one of many approaches underway at different companies.</p>
<p>There are three configurations for bringing fiber out (see figure 3, below). One takes the light from a waveguide, reflects it upward 90°, and sends it horizontally to a connector. These connectors would be situated around the periphery of the die.</p>
<p>Another is simply to run the waveguide to the edge of the die and have a connector butt up to the die to capture the light and route it to a fiber. The number of such connections is also determined by the perimeter.</p>
<p>A third approach moves the light vertically but then connects to the top surface rather than going out horizontally. This makes the entire area of the chip available for connections, providing more “shoreline” than the other two schemes.</p>
<p>“You have the waveguide shooting out laterally, and then it hits a mirror at a 45° angle,” said John. “It shoots upward, and once you get it upward, you put a connector with all the fibers on it.”</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24284404" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.52.45-AM.png?resize=1972%2C1110&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="1972" height="1110" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.52.45-AM.png?w=1972&amp;ssl=1 1972w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.52.45-AM.png?resize=300%2C169&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.52.45-AM.png?resize=1024%2C576&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.52.45-AM.png?resize=768%2C432&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.52.45-AM.png?resize=1536%2C865&amp;ssl=1 1536w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.52.45-AM.png?resize=600%2C338&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /><br />
<strong>Fig. 3: The three ways of getting a waveguide signal into a fiber — horizontal-to-vertical-to-horizontal, fully horizontal, and horizontal-to-vertical. Source: Bryon Moyer/Semiconductor Engineering</strong></p>
<p>But vertical approaches must avoid any heat sinks or other elements attached to the top. “When you send it vertically, you may get in the way of your thermal solution,” said Ravi Mahajan, fellow at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/intel-foundry/"><span style="font-weight: normal !msorm;">Intel Foundry</span></a>. “So if you have a solution that does both vertical and lateral, then you have more flexibility. In cases where thermal is a potential problem, go horizontal. In cases where you don&#8217;t worry about it, you can go vertical.”</p>
<p><strong>The problem with pigtails</strong><br />
As depicted above, these connectors could be short pieces of fiber — pigtails — that are affixed firmly to the die and with some sort of clip connector at the end. A matching connector clip could then be plugged in to make the connection. Such a clip would likely cause some insertion loss, but if managed well, it can work. And it can be made to be sturdy.</p>
<p>“We have taken a part with a pigtail and banged it on the desk to convince a VP that it doesn&#8217;t come off,” said Mahajan. “We expose it to temperature cycles, to a little bit of moisture, and bake it for a long time, and it doesn&#8217;t come off.”</p>
<p>But the greater concerns are the high-volume manufacturing implications. Even without considering how the fiber attaches to the die, a more immediate question is how these chips would be packaged in bulk for test and delivery. The same issue holds for shipping a longer fiber and cutting it to order during integration.</p>
<p>Today, chips go into trays, and automated handling equipment can pick and place them onto boards or into test heads or anywhere else they’re needed. What happens if it’s no longer just a chip in the tray pocket, but potentially a chip wrapped with pigtails?</p>
<p>“If you have a pigtail, it’s very difficult to wrap that pigtail around and put it in a tray, and you probably won&#8217;t have many parts on the tray,” said John. “So our choice is to have a detachable connector, and it also makes sense from a manufacturing perspective.”</p>
<p>In addition, it is inconvenient to try to place a chip with pigtails into such a tray in an orderly, repeatable way. And then, when pulling the chip out of the tray, it will have dangling pigtails floating about, potentially interfering with whatever operation the equipment is attempting. Then, presumably, those pigtails would be manually connected.</p>
<p>This arrangement, while suitable at low volumes, may not work in high-volume production.</p>
<p>“I understand people being worried about a pigtail dangling off the edge, so we decided, ‘Why not just bring it to the edge of the die,’” said Mahajan. “Then an integrator just attaches it [to the fiber].”</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24284405" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.54.38-AM.png?resize=988%2C870&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="988" height="870" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.54.38-AM.png?w=988&amp;ssl=1 988w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.54.38-AM.png?resize=300%2C264&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.54.38-AM.png?resize=768%2C676&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.54.38-AM.png?resize=600%2C528&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 988px) 100vw, 988px" /><br />
<strong>Fig. 4: A fiber plugs into the side of a package. Source: Intel Foundry</strong></p>
<p>Old habits change slowly, however. “Pigtails are probably still more common because people are trying to make incremental changes,” noted Sanjay Gangadhara, senior architect at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/synopsys-inc/"><span style="font-weight: normal !msorm;">Synopsys</span></a>.</p>
<p>Scaling volumes also must address the assembly of any connector on the package. “Terminating a fiber is complex,” said Badri Gomatam, CTO of <span style="font-weight: normal !msorm;">STL</span>. “You&#8217;ve got to make it low cost, high yield, and repeatable. You&#8217;ve got hundreds of connections. No speck of dust can be allowed, and the assembly has to survive high-temperature reflow and all the other growth steps.”</p>
<p><strong>Alignment concerns</strong><br />
One of the unique and challenging characteristics of photonics is that where two conductors meet, as much light as possible must get from one to the other. That applies to assembly and test.</p>
<p>Today, this is done through active alignment, where a tool measures the light exiting a waveguide and positions a fiber to maximize the captured light. This is a six-degree-of-freedom problem, where the aligner must be able to move in three linear dimensions as well as three angles of approach. It can take time.</p>
<p>With the pigtail configuration, presumably the clips would be designed to provide the alignment, but you still need to affix the stationary end of the pigtail during assembly. Given the downsides of pigtails, an alternative is to build the connector into the package itself. That way, nothing is hanging off the package, and it can be handled like other chips.</p>
<p>But alignment is still necessary. One method places a lens over the optical TSV to focus the light into an attached fiber while the other connection hardware aligns.</p>
<p>“One of the advantages when you have a lens is that, even if you do have some misalignment, the lens can correct for some of that because it’s focusing the light into a common region,” said Synopsys&#8217; Gangadhara. “So this idea of using micro-optics, whether in a vertical-coupling or an edge-coupling architecture, is becoming more and more common.”</p>
<p>The Senko technique, for instance, provides passive mechanical fine alignment and restraint when snapped together. Roy explained that the snap-on structure provides the fine alignment, but you need something to hold it down. “We have screws and a structure that pushes it down into the fine alignment. This is the mechanical latching.”</p>
<p>It’s one thing to perform alignment during assembly, but it’s another to require it when attaching system fibers to the chip. Magnetic connectors, such as those used by Apple on iPhones, are being considered. Once the receiver end is built into the package, someone (or an automated handler) attaching the fiber must first align it roughly in place. From there, the magnet pulls it into the necessary precise location.</p>
<p>Unfortunately, some magnetic connectors are easily damaged. Any acceptable CPO fiber connection must be firm.</p>
<p>Stronger magnets could help, and this is an active area of development, possibly along with a mechanical restraint (such as Senko’s screws). What’s unclear, however, is how strong that magnetic field would be and whether it may adversely affect surrounding components such as MRAMs.</p>
<p>“When you design your system board, are you going to put sensitive memory close to these devices?” mused John.</p>
<p>As a result, any mechanical alignment would likely also be supplemented with a latch.</p>
<p><strong>Serviceability and testability are critical</strong><br />
One way to connect fibers, common in long-haul optical applications, is to literally fuse them together. This permanent attachment method does not allow detachment, so it provides poor serviceability if a photonics chip fails.</p>
<p>“People have this concern about any kind of failure,&#8221; said Gangadhara. &#8220;How do you replace it? That serviceability part of the RAS requirement becomes a challenge.”</p>
<p>Serviceability, reliability, and availability make up the RAS feature that’s in high demand these days. It means components at greater risk of failure must be easy to replace, so you don&#8217;t have to throw out an entire expensive module or server.</p>
<p>“Once you attach a pigtail, if it gets damaged during shipment, the whole thing needs to be thrown out,” said John.</p>
<p>Roy agreed. “You need to make it detachable because if you permanently attach it and the fiber breaks, you have to fail the entire module out, which is very expensive,” he said.</p>
<p>Testing is heavily affected by these connector types, especially without standards. “An important aspect is making sure your solution is testable in real life,” noted Roy.</p>
<p>Addressing testability, especially for future high-volume manufacturing, means collaborating with test-equipment manufacturers. “We worked with Advantest and developed a test connector made out of tungsten carbide,” explained Roy. “It has the same bumps [as the LEGO-like connector] on the other side, and so it can come down, align precisely, and test very quickly. And you can scale it to test one at a time or a row of these things.”</p>
<p>Critically, the connection doesn’t require active alignment, saving test time. “We demonstrated 10,000 matings with very little variation in the loss [through the connection],” said Roy.</p>
<p>The connectors themselves must be reliable enough, but only up to a point. “Ideally, you&#8217;re not detaching and reattaching frequently,” noted Gangadhara. “If these things are up and running and working, then they&#8217;re running for thousands or tens of thousands of hours. Meta published a study last year where they had done serviceability/reliability analysis of some CPO devices, and they showed 10,000 hours of constant usage without any degradation of signal integrity or performance.”</p>
<p><strong>Settling on a common design</strong><br />
Connector attachment is a textbook case of a development that needs standards so that any user can plug into anyone else’s photonics chip. At present, there are numerous pigtail-based designs and no standards. Likewise, no magnetic (or other) pigtail-less approach has been standardized or proven in high volumes. Customer requirements also appear to vary in ways that may make standardization difficult.</p>
<p>Consider the mechanical latching of Senko’s solution, for example. “Each of these parts is custom, depending on the ganging requirements and the mechanical sizes that are important to avoid — cooling plates and thermal elements and such,&#8221; Roy said. “Our vision is to take the receptacle part [that gets attached to the package], make that a common interface of the chip, and then sell the cover plate to any FAU manufacturer.&#8221;</p>
<p>For that to become an actual standard, however, typical licensing will presumably need to be reasonable and non-discriminatory (RAND). That&#8217;s a situation where different connector companies will work closely with photonics companies to find an approach that works functionally and is suited to high-volume manufacturing. That means compatibility with handling equipment.</p>
<p>These efforts will likely continue until standards (or at least multi-source agreements, or MSAs) are put in place, removing one major photonics volume barrier once the solution is established.</p>
<hr />

		<title>Can AI Make Its Own Manufacturing Decisions?</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/can-ai-make-its-own-manufacturing-decisions/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/can-ai-make-its-own-manufacturing-decisions/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Gregory Haley]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 19 Nov 2026 08:01:02 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Manufacturing, Packaging and Materials]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Special Reports]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[advanced packaging]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[agentic AI]]></category>

		<category><![CDATA[artificial intelligence]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[ASE]]></category>

		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24284411</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>AI can optimize processes and find defects, but autonomous decisions require better data and strict process controls.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/can-ai-make-its-own-manufacturing-decisions/">Can AI Make Its Own Manufacturing Decisions?</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p><strong>Key Takeaways:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>AI can identify promising process conditions and recognize manufacturing problems, but those results still require physical validation before they can be trusted in production.</li>
<li>Incomplete manufacturing data and uncertainty about downstream effects make it difficult to give AI systems authority over process adjustments.</li>
<li>Greater manufacturing autonomy will require clear decision boundaries, failure containment, and ways to qualify models that change as they learn.</li>
</ul>
<hr />
<p>Semiconductor manufacturing has always depended on engineers making decisions involving complex interactions among process parameters. An etch recipe, for example, may involve combinations of RF power, pressure, gas chemistry, temperature, and timing, each affecting multiple process characteristics. Changing one parameter might improve critical dimension control while creating problems elsewhere. But as devices and manufacturing processes become more complicated, the growing number of variables involved makes those decisions increasingly difficult.</p>
<p>AI-driven manufacturing offers a way to manage some of that complexity, from finding better process conditions to identifying defects and responding to problems on the production line. But how much production responsibility engineers are willing to give those systems remains an open question, and one that drew considerable attention at SEMICON West 2026.</p>
<p>“No matter how good we are as engineers, no matter how disciplined and objective we are, we tend to approach these difficult problems with historical biases,” said David Fried, chief AI officer and corporate vice president of Semiverse Solutions at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/lam-research/">Lam Research</a> during a conference panel. “A process engineer might look at a set of data and say, ‘I need to move into a lower-pressure, higher-power regime because that’s how we solved this problem 10 years ago.’ But with this massive parameter space, AI can point us in a different direction, into an operating region we might never have considered because we couldn’t see those relationships in the data before.”</p>
<p><strong>Finding a process that keeps working</strong><br />
Finding an unfamiliar operating condition is useful, but determining whether it will hold up in production is another matter. Two recipes may produce similar results during development, yet respond quite differently to changes in incoming materials or equipment conditions. AI can help engineers identify those differences before committing to a particular process.</p>
<p>“It’s not just a question of delivering to a particular performance point. It’s also a question of how stable that particular performance point is, how robust it is,” said Vikas Sharma, senior principal engineer and senior director of pathfinding for <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/intel-foundry/">Intel Foundry</a> R&amp;D, in a recent presentation. “Minor perturbations in the incoming material or at the tool can lead to a significant drop in performance.”</p>
<p>The scale of the optimization problem helps explain why AI-driven manufacturing is attracting so much interest. Fried described a current Lam Research etcher with a potential recipe space on the order of 10²³ combinations. At Intel, Sharma discussed a process involving 350 parameters and approximately 10⁷⁰⁰ theoretical combinations.</p>
<p>According to Sharma, engineers working with equipment suppliers spent about a year developing a minimally acceptable recipe for that particular tool and process layer, followed by an extended effort to improve it with limited success. After introducing AI-assisted optimization, they began finding better results within a few months, eventually identifying multiple solutions that improved on the original baseline.</p>
<p>The challenge was determining which solutions were worth pursuing. Sharma noted that AI could identify thousands of candidates, but evaluating them still required expensive silicon experiments. Engineers also had to account for nonlinear relationships among parameters, which could change considerably across different operating regions.</p>
<p>“AI is not a magic wand. You can’t use the same AI solution for every problem,” Fried said. “Figuring out whether you’re using full physical simulation, surrogate models, machine learning, deep learning, LLM-based AI, or agentic AI, which of these solutions you’re building in which form to solve which problem and address which workflow, that’s more of what we’re spending our time on than actually building it or implementing it.”</p>
<p>The different approaches serve different purposes, from exploring process conditions to coordinating engineering workflows. Combining them may allow engineers to investigate more possibilities, but it also raises questions about how the results should be validated and whether the assumptions used in one model remain appropriate when its recommendations are passed to another.</p>
<p><strong>When the factory acts on what it sees</strong><br />
Inspection is one of the more established applications of AI in manufacturing, particularly in advanced packaging, where finer pitches and more complicated structures have increased the number of connections that must be examined. Some packages can contain hundreds of thousands of interconnects, each presenting a potential failure point.</p>
<p>Automated optical inspection and X-ray systems collect extensive information about those structures. Machine learning can identify variations that might be difficult to distinguish visually, but manufacturers still need to determine which differences indicate actual defects.</p>
<p>“When we look at X-rays, everybody just sees all shades of gray, but AI can see a thousand shades of gray,” said Ingu Yin Chang, executive vice president at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/ase/">ASE Group</a>. “But the AI only knows if this shade of gray equates to a problem once we say, ‘Hey, this equates to a problem.’ So, you’ve got to have the physical data that says, ‘Okay, that is a problem.’ Then, in the future, they can scan for that shade of gray and that location.”</p>
<p>Once manufacturers determine which image characteristics correspond to actual defects, AI-enabled inspection systems can flag abnormalities during production and trigger predefined equipment responses. At ASE, systems can recognize known abnormalities and trigger equipment responses before additional assemblies are affected.</p>
<p>“We have figured out how to do a lot of automation and use AI to help us identify potential issues, based on the issues that we physically learned through traditional manufacturing methods,” Chang said. “We will know when a wire bond is not bonded correctly, and the machine will immediately stop. There’s constant AI oversight to ask, ‘Are we doing everything correctly? Are we seeing it as we planned to see it?’”</p>
<p>Stopping equipment when a defect is detected can prevent additional losses, but it doesn’t necessarily mean AI is controlling the process independently. The inspection system may identify an abnormal condition while a conventional controller executes a predefined response. Allowing AI to select new bonding parameters and resume production would require considerably more confidence in its decisions.</p>
<p>ASE’s product diversity makes that challenge more difficult. Chang noted that hundreds of package types can pass through the same production lines with different materials, structures, and process sensitivities. A model would have to recognize which product is being manufactured and whether the conditions used during training remain applicable.</p>
<p>An inspection system might correctly identify a defective bond, but changing the process to prevent another failure requires knowing what caused it and whether an adjustment will create problems elsewhere. Manufacturers need considerably more information before allowing AI to make those decisions independently, and much of what’s needed remains difficult to obtain.</p>
<p><strong>Proving the model is right</strong><br />
One of the first obstacles to AI-driven manufacturing is establishing whether an AI recommendation accurately represents the physical process. A model can identify statistical relationships among manufacturing parameters without necessarily capturing the physical mechanisms behind them. That may be sufficient for finding promising operating conditions, but it becomes a greater concern when the system is expected to make decisions that affect production.</p>
<p>Sharma’s Intel example illustrates the problem. Pressure and temperature might dominate the response in one operating region, while voltage or gas flow could become more important elsewhere. A model trained on measurements from one region cannot assume those relationships will hold across another.</p>
<p>“The fundamental principle is that surrogate models are reliable when interpolating within the region represented by their training data and less reliable when extrapolating beyond it,” said Eric Guichard, senior vice president and general manager of AI manufacturing in <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/silvaco-inc/">Silvaco’s</a> TCAD business unit.</p>
<p>Finding an unfamiliar operating region may be exactly what makes AI useful, but it also means engineers have less experimental evidence against which to check its predictions. AI can identify thousands of candidate solutions, leaving engineers to determine which ones justify expensive silicon experiments. Before those recommendations can influence production, manufacturers need to establish where the predictions are reliable and where additional measurements are necessary.</p>
<p>Similar limitations appear in advanced packaging inspection, where a model may distinguish subtle differences in X-ray images without sufficient physical evidence to determine which indicate actual defects. A confident prediction from incomplete information becomes particularly risky when AI is authorized to act on it. An inappropriate adjustment could affect material that continues through several manufacturing steps before the problem is discovered.</p>
<p><strong>When the necessary data doesn’t exist</strong><br />
Semiconductor manufacturers have collected process and equipment data for decades, but much of it remains distributed among separate systems. Equipment logs, inspection records, and engineering databases may contain information about the same product without being connected in ways that make the relationships easy to analyze.</p>
<p>“The growing data challenge was there for us prior to AI,” Fried said. “Five to ten years ago, we really started rolling up our sleeves and building integrated data frameworks for our companies. Those of us who invested earlier and more heavily in building those data frameworks are reaping the benefits of AI now more quickly. Maybe AI has turbocharged this problem, but we’ve had this massive, growing, and complex interwoven web of data for quite some time now.”</p>
<p>Connecting existing data is difficult enough, but manufacturers also face situations where the information needed for a prediction was never measured or isn’t available from the company that generated it. Advanced packaging makes this especially challenging because manufacturing information may originate with several suppliers. A foundry provides the die, another company produces the substrate, and an OSAT performs assembly and test, often without everyone having access to the same manufacturing history.</p>
<p>Some of the most difficult gaps involve physical properties that change during manufacturing. Substrate warpage, thermal stress, and material behavior can affect subsequent operations, particularly as assemblies move through repeated thermal cycles. Those changes aren’t always captured in the available measurements, and a model relying on nominal material properties may miss them entirely.</p>
<p>Manufacturers also need to preserve the assumptions behind the data they exchange. A model developed for one operating range may produce results that appear reasonable when used elsewhere, even though the original calibration no longer applies.</p>
<p>“If the training range, calibration source, or approved use is not carried with the model, it may be good enough for early exploration but not safe for design closure,” said Kenneth Larsen, senior director of product management at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/synopsys-inc/">Synopsys</a>. “The missing information is not just more data; it is the context that tells the next tool or team how the data should be used.”</p>
<p>As AI systems begin coordinating decisions across multiple process steps, those gaps become more consequential. A recommendation based on one operation may overlook conditions affecting the next, particularly when the model lacks information about the product’s actual physical state. Additional metrology and characterization may help close some of those gaps, but engineers still have to establish whether the available information is sufficient to act on a recommendation.</p>
<p><strong>Who decides what AI can do?</strong><br />
Even with reliable models and better data, manufacturers still have to determine which decisions should be delegated to AI. Existing equipment automation generally operates within established procedures and control limits, but an AI agent capable of evaluating conditions and selecting corrective actions raises questions about who authorizes those decisions and what happens when something goes wrong.</p>
<p>An agent investigating a process problem might need to retrieve inspection results, examine equipment histories, and evaluate possible corrective actions using several engineering tools. Each activity may involve different access permissions, particularly when the information comes from separate facilities or manufacturing partners. Access to those resources also needs to be distinguished from the authority to initiate changes.</p>
<p>“There is a question of data access and data consolidation, and there is also a question of data interpretation,” Sharma said. “But the problem that AI has brought forth as a novel thing, which was not as big an issue before, is governance. When agents are working, how do you ensure that the appropriate privileges for the right data are available to the right agent and accessible to the right person?”</p>
<p>An agent might be authorized to examine process records and recommend an adjustment without having permission to modify a recipe or restart equipment. Those restrictions become harder to enforce when agents coordinate activities across multiple systems, particularly if one tool can initiate actions through another. Access to one system cannot automatically confer authority over every subsequent operation.</p>
<p>Even an authorized adjustment may carry risks. Changing bonding force or temperature, for example, might correct an immediate defect while affecting adjacent connections or downstream reliability. Engineers would have to establish which adjustments can be made within qualified operating ranges, when approval is required, and how the system should respond if a proposed correction falls outside its validated capabilities.</p>
<p>If an AI-directed correction produces an unexpected result, equipment may need to stop while affected material is held for investigation. Manufacturers would also need to establish whether the system should attempt another correction, return control to an engineer, or prevent subsequent operations from proceeding. Those decisions have to be defined before autonomous adjustments are permitted.</p>
<p>Future agentic architectures will likely rely on agents reviewing and verifying one another’s work. That could provide another level of oversight, although the interactions themselves would need to be controlled. Agents relying on the same incomplete information might reinforce one another’s mistakes, and their collective actions could have consequences that none of the individual systems anticipated.</p>
<p>Recent research into agentic AI has demonstrated cases in which systems pursued assigned objectives through actions their operators never intended, including circumventing restrictions or concealing information. [1, 2] Although the research includes controlled experimental scenarios and incidents involving coding agents, it raises questions about how multiple agents might behave when working together.</p>
<p>In manufacturing, a series of individually permitted adjustments could collectively move a process beyond its qualified operating conditions, even if no single action violated its assigned limits. Preventing that would require controls over the combined effects of agent decisions, along with independent verification that the resulting process remains within acceptable limits.</p>
<p>Manufacturing traceability adds another requirement. Engineers investigating a later failure must be able to reconstruct what an agent observed, which alternatives it evaluated, why it selected a particular action, and what material was affected. Those records would need to connect the model’s decisions with equipment settings and manufacturing history.</p>
<p>The amount of authority manufacturers can safely delegate will depend on how reliably AI can operate within those boundaries. A system may be capable of choosing a correction, but it also needs to recognize when the available information is insufficient or when the consequences of an adjustment cannot be predicted with enough confidence.</p>
<p>Maintaining those limits becomes even more complicated when AI models are updated with new production data, potentially changing their behavior after the original operating boundaries have been established.</p>
<p><strong>Qualifying a system that keeps learning</strong><br />
A semiconductor fab may process thousands of wafers between updates to a particular AI model. During that time, equipment conditions change, new material arrives, and engineers collect information that could improve the models’ predictions. Incorporating those results more quickly would be valuable, but manufacturers also need to know whether an updated model will behave as reliably as the one it replaces.</p>
<p>“We are far, far, far away from continuous learning,” Sharma said. “We do episodic learning, once every quarter a model is changed, or once every month a model is changed. But if a factory is running 10,000 wafers, we have 10,000 wafers times whatever number of weeks in between those model changes. How are we using that data in a way that rapidly applies to the learning of that particular model or the agent, or both?”</p>
<p>More frequent updates would introduce additional qualification work. A model might become better at recognizing one process excursion while becoming less reliable under conditions represented in its previous training data. If that model can adjust equipment settings, engineers need to establish what changed and whether the updated behavior remains within qualified limits.</p>
<p>Keeping track of those changes also requires more than identifying which software version was running. Engineers need to preserve the model’s training and validation history, along with the process conditions under which it was qualified. If an AI system changes its behavior during production, investigating a failure weeks later may require reconstructing the model as it existed when a particular lot was processed.</p>
<p>While manufacturers already have procedures for qualifying process changes and software revisions, an AI system that updates itself during production would have to fit within those controls, potentially requiring restrictions on what the model can change and independent checks before revised behavior affects equipment. Changes in manufacturing conditions would also need to be distinguished from changes introduced by retraining the model itself.</p>
<p>The harder question is whether manufacturers can obtain the benefits of continuous learning without having to repeat an extensive qualification process after every update. AI can already help engineers make better manufacturing decisions, but giving it responsibility for those decisions will require evidence that its behavior remains predictable and that its actions can be traced back to validated process conditions.</p>
<p><strong>The path to greater autonomy</strong><br />
Semiconductor manufacturers have spent decades developing processes that can produce consistent results across equipment, materials, and production environments. AI introduces new possibilities for improving those processes, but it also introduces uncertainty about how decisions are made and whether the results can be reproduced. The same standards of process control, qualification, and traceability will have to apply when software begins making decisions that engineers previously handled themselves.</p>
<p>Greater autonomy is likely to emerge gradually, beginning with decisions that can be made within well-characterized operating limits and expanding as manufacturers gain confidence in the systems controlling them. AI may eventually identify a problem, determine an appropriate correction, and verify the result without engineering intervention. Getting there will depend on whether manufacturers can demonstrate that those decisions remain reliable as equipment conditions, materials, and the models themselves change.</p>
<p><strong>References</strong></p>
<ol>
<li>A. Lynch, J. Hughes, A. Serrano, R. Kirk, and S. R. Bowman, “Agentic Misalignment in Summer 2026,” Anthropic, July 13, 2026. <a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/agentic-misalignment-summer-2026/">https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/agentic-misalignment-summer-2026/</a></li>
<li>OpenAI, “Suppressing Misalignment Monitoring Alerts and Disabling Misalignment Monitoring,” OpenAI Alignment, October 20, 2026. <a href="https://alignment.openai.com/misalignment-reports/suppressing-misalignment-monitoring-alerts-and-disabling-misalignment-monitoring/">https://alignment.openai.com/misalignment-reports/suppressing-misalignment-monitoring-alerts-and-disabling-misalignment-monitoring/</a></li>
</ol>

		<item>
		<title>Can AI Agents Really Shift Chip Design Left?</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/can-ai-agents-really-shift-chip-design-left/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/can-ai-agents-really-shift-chip-design-left/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Ed Sperling]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 18 Nov 2026 08:01:53 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Round Tables]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[System-Level Design]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Top Stories]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[agentic AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Axiomise]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Hiveware]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[shift left]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Silvaco]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Synopsys]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24284337</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Agentic AI promises to automate more of the chip flow, but integration with EDA tools and capturing decades of engineering expertise are non-trivial.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/can-ai-agents-really-shift-chip-design-left/">Can AI Agents Really Shift Chip Design Left?</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p><strong>Key Takeaways:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>Agent orchestration at various stages of design, verification, and validation will require new methodologies and coordination across tools.</li>
<li>Engineering expertise remains essential, and proprietary data is still siloed.</li>
<li>Cost and complexity could limit adoption, giving larger companies an advantage.</li>
</ul>
<hr />
<p><strong>Experts at the Table</strong>: AI agents are moving into more parts of the chip design flow, but connecting them across different tools and processes remains a challenge. Questions also remain about how to capture decades of engineering expertise, protect proprietary design data, and keep costs manageable. Semiconductor Engineering sat down to discuss these issues with Frank Schirrmeister, executive director of strategy programs and system solutions at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/synopsys-inc/">Synopsys</a>; Ronen Laviv, vice president of business development and strategic partnerships at Hiveware; Jack Berg, vice president of business development at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/silvaco-inc/">Silvaco</a>; Ashish Darbari, founder and CEO of <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/axiomise/">Axiomise</a>. What follows are excerpts of that conversation. To view part one of this discussion, click <a href="https://semiengineering.com/rethinking-shift-left-in-chip-design/">here</a>. Part two is <a href="https://semiengineering.com/shift-left-is-becoming-a-system-level-problem/">here</a>.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283440" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Screenshot-2026-10-28-at-3.04.41-PM.png?resize=1716%2C538&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="1716" height="538" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Screenshot-2026-10-28-at-3.04.41-PM.png?w=1716&amp;ssl=1 1716w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Screenshot-2026-10-28-at-3.04.41-PM.png?resize=300%2C94&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Screenshot-2026-10-28-at-3.04.41-PM.png?resize=1024%2C321&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Screenshot-2026-10-28-at-3.04.41-PM.png?resize=768%2C241&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Screenshot-2026-10-28-at-3.04.41-PM.png?resize=1536%2C482&amp;ssl=1 1536w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Screenshot-2026-10-28-at-3.04.41-PM.png?resize=600%2C188&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /><br />
<strong>L-R: Silvaco’s Berg; Synopsys’ Schirrmeister; Axiomise’s Darbari; Hiveware’s Laviv.</strong></p>
<p><strong>SE: If AI agents are modifying code, and you keep updating AI models and agents, how much can you really shift left?</strong></p>
<p><strong>Darbari</strong>: This is going to be a new methodology direction the industry will be taking, much like UVM, where you combine these different flows together. How do you combine all the agents together? I don&#8217;t get a sense that anybody really knows today how this combination is going to work. From what I have seen in different presentations, they&#8217;re still looking at how these things unfold. A lot of agentic AI companies lack the field experience of having seen these problems firsthand, so what they&#8217;re building is not what the consumer wants. In many cases, the big consumers are building their own stuff because they know what they want. They have the field experience. They have data from many years. So, where will the big EDA vendors go to streamline this, and how will that shape the end use? I don&#8217;t know.</p>
<p><strong>Laviv</strong>: One of the challenges with emulation is that when running in co-simulation mode, the simulation slows down. Maybe, instead of having UVM or other languages that slow it down, AI will get its own language and get SystemVerilog RTL synthesizable code that you can put in the machine. Then you&#8217;ll need less of the software, and you can run faster in emulation. I&#8217;m not sure yet if the methodology would change quickly, but UVM might not stay forever.</p>
<p><strong>Darbari</strong>: That&#8217;s what Nvidia&#8217;s head of GPU said at DVCon U.S., isn&#8217;t it? The formal is replacing simulation, or is going to replace simulation. I&#8217;m less interested in how each of these individual technologies works with each other, but I&#8217;m very interested in whether all of this adoption will lead to a coherent story.</p>
<p><strong>Schirrmeister</strong>: You have lifecycle, manufacturing, digital implementation, functional verification, and you have specification engineering. Making sense of it is the light bulb — making sure it works, getting it to layout, getting it into the actual physical silicon and 3D, and then observing it during the lifecycle. Each of these flows has many sub-components. Functional verification to emulation is like one of the five. There is virtual prototyping, formal, simulation, emulation, and prototyping. Everybody gets their own agent. So you have an agent doing something localized. Now you start getting an agent that orchestrates these things together as a functional thing, and then you get an agent that connects to the front end. This is an onion of agents, with more and more layers on top. EDA vendors are all saying we have Level 5 stuff, and there&#8217;s truth to that within the scope of the operating domain.</p>
<p><strong>Berg</strong>: This is very much about hierarchy.</p>
<p><strong>Darbari</strong>: But who&#8217;s going to pay for all this? Do the small companies have the budget to tokenize their banks and run all of this?</p>
<p><strong>Schirrmeister</strong>: Do you mean the guys building the product, or the guys building the tools?</p>
<p><strong>Darbari</strong>: The ones running the product. These are the consumers. They would need to pay for the tokens. I have a feeling that only the big companies will be able to afford this. They&#8217;ve been running it full-on, saying they haven&#8217;t written any code. All of this is agents. But it would get very interesting if the cost of doing all of this could be significantly reduced.</p>
<p><strong>Schirrmeister</strong>: What&#8217;s exciting about this is that all the smaller designs that used to be difficult because you had to go through all of this will benefit. If you want to be an Nvidia competitor, you still cannot do this out of your garage. That still has to be a big company.</p>
<p><strong>Berg</strong>: You&#8217;ve got levels of agents at this point, and a Level 5 orchestrating the whole thing. The industry is going to work on building each of these and then iterating and improving them. That&#8217;s the only way in which you&#8217;re going to get agents to learn and replace large groups of engineers doing the grunt work that&#8217;s necessary here.</p>
<p><strong>SE: But that hierarchy needs to start with whatever shifts left to include in each one of these different blocks, right? You need to at least define, in general, what you&#8217;re going to be doing.</strong></p>
<p><strong>Berg</strong>: You&#8217;re going to have information being fed back to ensure that when they go to the specification of the functional, there are constraints placed here. That way, you can improve the probability of manufacturability.</p>
<p><strong>Schirrmeister</strong>: Can I have an agent model and predict that path?</p>
<p><strong>Berg</strong>: Yes, you can.</p>
<p><strong>Schirrmeister</strong>: But you want to check, right? You need to make sure early on what the multi-physics effects of what you&#8217;re doing will be.</p>
<p><strong>Berg</strong>: Yes, and without that, it will be dead on arrival in manufacturing.</p>
<p><strong>Schirrmeister</strong>: So now the question becomes, &#8216;How much can you really abstract?&#8217;</p>
<p><strong>Laviv</strong>: AI is doing a great job on, say, 80% of the challenges. But then you have experience from individuals who have been in the industry for 30 years with unique skills that AI doesn&#8217;t have. We had a discussion where we talked about 250 different skills for power, emulation, format, and verification. AI doesn&#8217;t have those skills today. The industry needs to figure out how to gather those skills in order to really get to a flow where an agent can talk to an agent and has all the decades of experience that is hidden in Apple, Qualcomm, and Intel.</p>
<p><strong>Berg</strong>: It took 50 years, but in 2024, the Nobel Prize in chemistry was given in part to a software called <a href="https://deepmind.google/science/alphafold/">AlphaFold</a>, which predicted with uncanny certainty how proteins fold. Proteins are much more complicated than the crystalline structures that we deal with on a daily basis. But it took 50 years to get there. That said, it&#8217;s a proof of concept. Whether or not you believe it can be done in semiconductors, it&#8217;s going to happen.</p>
<p><strong>Laviv</strong>: The difference is that with proteins, everything is open.</p>
<p><strong>Schirrmeister</strong>: Yes. Where does the data come from? Simon Davidmann (currently a professor at the University of Southampton) framed it as, &#8216;We have the big three. They have the base tools, and they build agent stacks on it. That&#8217;s why you have a chip stack, or super stack, or whatever.&#8217; We are doing the same thing at Synopsys, and we&#8217;ll see who has the best traditional EDA. People who know AI well found EDA. But they need to learn EDA first, rather than the other way around, and they don&#8217;t have access to the data they need. The users have access to the data. Samsung talked about optimizing the quality of service for a network-on-chip. They had a fast classic engine, an SoC simulator, an emulator, and they built their own LLM to feed all the data in there. That&#8217;s their company data, and it&#8217;s not going out to anybody. That&#8217;s why they&#8217;re doing it themselves. Cognichip said you need LLMs that are trained with real chip designs and people, which is why they hired chip designers and built chips.</p>
<p><strong>SE: They&#8217;re also using that intelligence for the LLMs rather than the agents, right?</strong></p>
<p><strong>Schirrmeister</strong>: Exactly. And that&#8217;s the balance you need.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/can-ai-agents-really-shift-chip-design-left/">Can AI Agents Really Shift Chip Design Left?</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></content:encoded>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>PCIe and trust; rack-scale co-design; heterogeneous scaling; AI data center primer; on-device balancing; EU semi trends.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/blog-review-nov-18-3/">Blog Review: Nov. 18</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Cadence&#8217;s Rajesh Pamujula considers how to <a href="https://community.cadence.com/cadence_blogs_8/b/ip/posts/pci-express-security-and-tee-integration-part-1---establishing-trust">establish trust</a> when sensitive data, proprietary models, cryptographic keys, and confidential workloads increasingly traverse PCIe links.</p>
<p>Synopsys&#8217; Neeraj Paliwal argues that <a href="https://www.synopsys.com/blogs/chip-design/next-generation-ai-factories.html">extreme co-design</a>, where silicon, interface IP, memory, cooling, and power are designed together as one system, is needed for efficient racks that are capable of running increasingly demanding AI workloads.</p>
<p>Siemens&#8217; Kevin Rinebold and Andras Vass-Varnai examine the evolving semiconductor design landscape shaped by the shift from monolithic scaling to <a href="https://blogs.sw.siemens.com/semiconductor-packaging/2026/10/21/scaling-3d-ic-design-trends-challenges-and-practical-workflows-part-2/">heterogeneous architectures</a> and consider how to navigate the complexities of AI-driven 3D-IC and chiplet technologies.</p>
<p>Keysight&#8217;s Linas Dauksa provides a primer on the technologies that power <a href="https://www.keysight.com/blogs/en/tech/educ/ai-data-center">AI data centers</a>, how they handle training and inference workloads, and why they&#8217;ve become the backbone of modern AI.</p>
<p>Arm&#8217;s Michael Gamble checks out how to enable <a href="https://developer.arm.com/community/arm-community-blogs/b/ai-blog/posts/how-efficient-on-device-ai-can-make-smart-glasses-more-wearable">gaze-based</a> interactions in lightweight smart glasses by running fast, sensor-dependent AI on-device while larger workloads stay where more compute is available.</p>
<p>SEMI&#8217;s Sitong He chats with Patrick Vandenameele of imec about the <a href="https://www.semi.org/en/blogs/from-better-chips-to-better-systems-a-conversation-with-imec-ceo-patrick-vandenameele-ahead-of-semicon-europa-2026">trends reshaping</a> the semiconductor industry, why collaboration matters more than ever, and the role Europe can play in global competitiveness.</p>
<p>Plus, check out blogs featured in the latest <a href="https://semiengineering.com/newsletter/low-power-high-performance-nov-2026/">Low Power-High Performance newsletter</a>:</p>
<p>Rambus’ Kushal Koolwal examines why longer contexts, higher concurrency, multimodal models, and AI agents will continue changing where <a href="https://semiengineering.com/beyond-bandwidth-ai-inference-is-reshaping-the-memory-wall/">memory pressure</a> appears.</p>
<p>Siemens EDA’s Jim Shiely shows how to use <a href="https://semiengineering.com/the-semiconductor-singularity-when-ai-starts-accelerating-the-silicon-that-accelerates-ai/">evidence from silicon</a> to improve the tools and decisions for next-gen hardware.</p>
<p>Synopsys’ Andrew Appleby observes that traditional <a href="https://semiengineering.com/designing-edge-ai-socs-at-near-threshold-why-foundation-ip-can-help-you-break-down-the-power-wall/">voltage scaling</a> is no longer sufficient to deliver real-time performance within tight energy and thermal limits.</p>
<p>Cadence’s Joe Chen demonstrates how to right-size the <a href="https://semiengineering.com/the-memory-challenge-of-physical-ai/">memory hierarchy</a> around the device’s data streams, reuse patterns, latency requirements, and operating constraints.</p>
<p>Arm’s Siyu Mou explains the rationale behind <a href="https://semiengineering.com/introduction-to-fp8-formats-part-1/">8-bit floating-point</a> data formats, their ecosystem momentum, and hardware support.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/blog-review-nov-18-3/">Blog Review: Nov. 18</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>

<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The need for significant investment in R&amp;D is nothing new for memory makers, but Micron Technology’s creation of a dedicated facility that looks beyond the next node illustrates why the industry cannot</p>]]>

					<post-id xmlns="com-wordpress:feed-additions:1">101803831</post-id>				</item>
							<item>
					<title>Gartner Warns of Risks Behind Sovereign Tech Claims</title>
					<link>https://www.eetimes.com/gartner-warns-of-risks-behind-sovereign-tech-claims/</link>

											<comments>https://www.eetimes.com/gartner-warns-of-risks-behind-sovereign-tech-claims/#respond</comments>
					
					<dc:creator>
						<![CDATA[Pablo Valerio]]>
					</dc:creator>
					<pubDate>Thu, 19 Nov 2026 16:30:35 +0000</pubDate>
							<category><![CDATA[Blog]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[News]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Supply Chain & Distribution]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[cybersecurity]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Digital Sovereignty]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[European DIgital]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Gartner]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[geopolitics]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Semiconductors]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Sovereign AI]]></category>
